Abstract
Patients undergoing hearing-preservation cochlear implantation (HPCI) often retain low-frequency thresholds initially, yet many develop progressive loss of residual hearing and variable speech performance. The biological basis of this delayed functional decline remains unclear. Here we show that HPCI at the basal turn induces primary neural degeneration at the cochlear apex—a region anatomically remote from the electrode array and functionally important for low-frequency hearing and speech discrimination. Using a mouse model of HPCI (all male), we systematically quantified longitudinal electrophysiological and histopathological changes up to 16 weeks after implantation. Although low-frequency thresholds were preserved and remained stable, suprathreshold auditory brainstem response wave I amplitudes progressively declined. Correspondingly, afferent synapses were progressively reduced across the cochlea, with synapse loss exceeding inner hair cell (IHC) loss by 7.1-fold at the cochlear apex, demonstrating neural degeneration independent of IHC death. Cochlear nerve fiber loss followed synapse loss and was consistent with progressive neural disconnection. Notably, apical synapse survival was best predicted by the slope of the wave I amplitude-level function, directly linking CI-induced primary neural degeneration to suprathreshold electrophysiological decline. Together, these findings demonstrate that HPCI can induce primary neural degeneration at the cochlear apex, providing a mechanistic explanation for the delayed loss of residual acoustic hearing and for functional deficits not captured by audiometric threshold measures.
cochlear implant
delayed loss of residual hearing
hearing-preservation cochlear implant
primary neural degeneration
synaptopathy
Significance Statement
We demonstrate that hearing-preservation cochlear implantation induces primary neural degeneration at the cochlear apex, a region anatomically remote from the electrode array and essential for low-frequency hearing and speech recognition. Using a longitudinal mouse model, we show that afferent synapse and cochlear nerve fiber loss progresses across the cochlea and markedly exceeds inner hair cell loss at the apex. This degeneration occurs despite preserved low-frequency thresholds and is best predicted by suprathreshold auditory brainstem response wave I measures, revealing neural dysfunction not detected by standard audiometry. These findings challenge the prevailing assumption that apical cochlear structures remain intact after basal electrode insertion.
Introduction
Cochlear implantation (CI) is an established therapy to restore hearing in patients with severe-to-profound sensorineural hearing loss and is increasingly performed in older adults (Nassiri et al., 2023), the majority of whom exhibit down-sloping audiograms characterized by preserved low-frequency hearing (Dubno et al., 2013; Vaden et al., 2017). These patients are therefore candidates for hearing-preservation CI (HPCI). Preservation of low-frequency acoustic hearing provides substantial functional benefit, particularly in difficult listening situations such as speech perception in noise and for music appreciation (Buchner et al., 2009; Gifford et al., 2013; Gantz et al., 2022). However, a significant proportion of patients experience progressive deterioration despite initially successful hearing preservation.
During implantation, the electrode array (EA) is inserted through the round window or a cochleostomy at the basal turn and advanced along the scala tympani toward the apex. Because the cochlea is tonotopically organized—with high frequencies represented at the base and low frequencies at the apex (von Békésy, 1960)—structures apical to the EA are presumed to remain anatomically and functionally intact after implantation. This assumption underlies the rationale for HPCI. Although preservation of low-frequency acoustic hearing following CI has been achieved across multiple centers (Gantz, 1998; Balkany et al., 2006; Roland et al., 2016), clinical outcomes are variable: up to 40% of HPCI patients lose their residual hearing either immediately after implantation or in a delayed fashion (Scheperle et al., 2017; Tarabichi et al., 2021; O’Malley et al., 2024). The biological mechanism driving this deterioration remains unclear.
Prior investigations of CI-induced pathology have largely focused on inflammatory responses, fibrosis, and new bone formation either (1) inferred from electrode impedances and electrocochleography (ECochG) measures (Scheperle et al., 2017; Shearer et al., 2018; Tejani et al., 2022), (2) visualized via ultrahigh-resolution CT (Heutink et al., 2022), (3) examined in postmortem temporal bones (Fayad et al., 1991; Kamakura and Nadol, 2016), or (4) extrapolated from animal models of CI (Claussen et al., 2022; Rahman et al., 2025). These pathologies are predominantly concentrated near the EA. In contrast, the effects of CI on the sensory epithelium and neural elements that encode acoustic information—particularly in regions tonotopically remote from the EA—remain poorly defined. Whether implantation disrupts afferent synapses and cochlear nerve fibers (CNFs) in apical regions—critical for low-frequency hearing—has not been systematically examined.
Here, using a longitudinal mouse model of HPCI, we systematically quantified electrophysiological and histological changes throughout the cochlear spiral following implantation. We identify a previously unrecognized consequence of CI: primary neural degeneration at the cochlear apex, a region anatomically distant from the EA. This phenomenon is characterized by profound afferent synapse loss that exceeds inner hair cell (IHC) loss, indicating neural degeneration independent of sensory cell death. This primary neural degeneration occurs despite preservation of low-frequency audiometric thresholds. Together, our findings challenge the prevailing assumption that apical structures remain biologically intact after basal EA insertion and provide a mechanistic framework linking CI to the delayed loss of residual acoustic hearing and associated functional deficits.
Materials and Methods
Animals and groups
Male CBA/CaJ mice were obtained at 7 weeks of age and acclimated for 1 week prior to surgery. At 8 weeks, animals underwent unilateral CI of the left ear. Three experimental cohorts were included (n = 6 per group), corresponding to survival times of 4 d, 8 weeks, and 16 weeks following implantation. The unimplanted contralateral ears served as age-matched controls. Because no systematic differences were observed across survival times, control ears from all cohorts were pooled for subsequent analysis. The cochlear implant (HL03, Cochlear Limited) consisted of a silicon matrix with three exposed platinum electrodes, designed to mimic the human CI but scaled for the mouse cochlea (overall diameter of the intracochlear array = 0.15 mm) to allow atraumatic insertion through the round window (Reiss et al., 2022). CI surgery was performed as described previously (Claussen et al., 2019). Briefly, animals were anesthetized with isoflurane and maintained at normothermia using a far-infrared warming pad (Kent Scientific). A postauricular incision was made, and the bulla was opened using a 0.5 mm otologic drill to expose the round window niche. The round window membrane was punctured with a fine platinum wire (0.075 mm diameter). The EA was then inserted through the round window to an insertion depth of 2.25 mm and secured to the bulla with 10-0 nonabsorbable sutures to preserve middle ear integrity. No electrical stimulation was delivered. Animals were allowed to recover on the warming pad and then returned to the animal care facility. All protocols and procedures were approved by the Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee of the University of Iowa and conformed to the guidelines in the NRC Guide for the Care and Use of Laboratory Animals.
Cochlear function tests
Animals underwent bilateral cochlear function testing immediately prior to tissue harvesting. For measuring auditory brainstem responses (ABRs) and distortion product otoacoustic emissions (DPOAEs), animals were anesthetized with xylazine (20 mg/kg, i.p.) and ketamine (100 mg/kg, i.p.) and placed in an acoustically and electrically shielded room. Body temperature was maintained at 37°C using Deltaphase Isothermal Pads (Braintree Scientific). ABRs were recorded via subdermal needle electrodes (vertex–ventrolateral to the pinna) using a Tucker-Davis Technologies system. Briefly, stimuli were generated by an RZ6 multi-I/O processor and delivered in a closed-field configuration via an MF1 magnetic speaker positioned at the opening of the external ear canal. ABR stimuli were 2 ms tone pips at 8–64 kHz with a 0.25 ms rise–fall time delivered at 21 Hz (alternating polarity). The response from the electrodes was amplified (Medusa4Z BioAmp), filtered (300–3 kHz), and averaged (512 samples) in the BioSigRZ software. The sound level was decreased from 90 dB sound pressure level (SPL) to 10 dB below threshold. Larger steps (10 dB) were taken at stimulus levels at least 20 dB above threshold; smaller steps (5 dB) were taken closer to threshold. The speaker was calibrated before each recording using a 0.25 inch calibrating microphone (Model 378C01, PCB Piezotronics). ABR threshold was defined visually by the examiner as the lowest stimulus level at which a repeatable waveform morphology could be identified in the response. The threshold was assigned to 100 dB SPL if no repeatable waveform could be identified at the highest stimulus level tested (90 dB SPL). Wave I amplitude was measured from the peak to the succeeding trough by offline analysis in MATLAB using the signal processing toolbox. DPOAEs were recorded using the OtoPhyLab system. The stimuli were two primary tones f1 and f2 (f2/f1 = 1.2) with the primary tone f2 set at 6.0, 8.0, 12.0, 16.0, 24.0, or 32.0 kHz. Primaries were swept in 5 dB steps from 20 to 70 dB SPL (equal SPLs for both f1 and f2). The DPOAE at 2f1–f2 was extracted from the ear canal sound pressure, digitized within the OtoPhyLab system. Threshold was computed by interpolation as the primary level (f2) required to produce a DPOAE above the noise floor. All analyses were performed with the examiner blinded to experimental group assignment.
Cochlear processing
Histological processing was carried out in batches, typically comprising five cochleae, and always included at least one control cochlea for setting confocal image acquisition parameters. The cochleae were fixed by intracardial perfusion using 4% paraformaldehyde in phosphate buffer, then immediately dissected, perfused through the scalae, postfixed for 2 h at room temperature, and then decalcified in EDTA. After decalcification, cochleae were permeabilized by cryoprotection in 30% sucrose, frozen on dry ice, thawed, rinsed in PBS, and then microdissected into six pieces (roughly half-turns of the cochlear spiral) for whole-mount processing of the organ of Corti. During whole-mount dissection, the cochlea was carefully examined under a dissection microscope for the fibrosis tissue—soft and pliable tissue filling the scala tympani—or translocation of the EA, present as a disruption of the basilar membrane. When present, the location and extent of the fibrosis tissue or translocation were estimated qualitatively. The fibrosis tissue, if present, was gently removed from the scala tympani before proceeding with cochlear dissection. The basilar membrane remained intact after removal. Immunostaining began with a blocking buffer (PBS with 5% normal goat serum and 0.3% Triton X-100) for 1 h at room temperature, followed by overnight incubation at 37°C, as previously described (Wu et al., 2024), with the following primary antibodies: (1) mouse (IgG1) anti-CtBP2 (C-terminal binding protein) from BD Biosciences at 1:200, to quantify presynaptic ribbons; (2) mouse (IgG2a) anti-GluA2 (glutamate receptor subunit A2) from Millipore at 1:2,000, to quantify postsynaptic receptor patches; (3) chicken anti-Neurofilament 200 (NF-200) from Chemicon at 1:500, to label CNFs; and (4) rabbit anti-Myosin VI/VIIa from Proteus BioSciences at 1:200, to delineate the hair cell cytoplasm. Primary incubations were followed by two sequential 60 min incubations at 37°C in species-appropriate secondary antibodies (coupled to Alexa Fluor dyes) with 0.3% Triton X-100.
Image acquisition and morphometric analysis
Two sets of confocal (Stellaris 5, Leica) z-stacks were captured from each cochlea: (1) low-power images captured using a glycerol-immersion objective (40×, N.A. 1.25), tiled to include the entire cochlear piece (1,744 × 1,744 pixels at 130 nm/pixel, 3 µm z-spacing), with z-planes spanning the height of the organ of Corti to capture all hair cells (HCs) in that piece, and (2) high-power images obtained using an oil-immersion objective (63×, N.A. 1.4) at 2.7 digital zoom (1,744 × 872 pixels at 39 nm/pixel, 0.30 µm z-spacing), captured at two adjacent locations at each half-octave cochlear frequency point from 5.6 to 64.0 kHz. Each z-stack contained 10–12 adjacent IHCs with z-planes spanning the height of the IHC to capture all synaptic puncta and the CNFs in that region of interest. Z-stacks were deconvolved using the Leica Lightning protocol. Quantitative analyses were conducted by an examiner blinded to experimental group assignment.
Cochlear frequency map (from low-power images): Maximum-intensity projections on the x–y plane (acquisition plane) were generated from low-power images. The cochlear length was then measured using custom MATLAB software and converted to cochlear frequency (kilohertz) using the cochlear map for the mouse (Taberner and Liberman, 2005).
Cytocochleogram (from low-power images): Hair cell analysis was aided by custom MATLAB software. The program locates the hair cells by identifying the peak pixel brightness along a curve corresponding to inner or outer hair cell (OHC) regions. Pixel intensities were averaged using a 7 × 7 pixel smoothing kernel to reduce noise. The automated results were then validated manually. The denominator for the fractional survival was derived from the mean hair cell value in control cochleae.
Afferent synapse count (from high-power images): A custom MATLAB pipeline using the bwconncomp function was employed to automatically identify presynaptic puncta in the z-stack and record their coordinates and counts. The custom MATLAB software was then used to extract 1 µm3 voxels centered on each ribbon's x, y, z coordinates and reprojected the voxel space onto the x–y plane and assembled a montage of thumbnail images (Liberman et al., 2011; Wu et al., 2024). To classify whether each presynaptic ribbon (visualized with anti-CtBP2) was paired with a postsynaptic receptor patch (visualized with anti-GluA2), the thumbnail images were sorted using an automatic machine-learning pipeline as described previously (Wu et al., 2024). The algorithm was validated within the current dataset using 818 manually labeled thumbnails, achieving a balanced accuracy of 93.3%. Results from two adjacent z-stacks were averaged and normalized to the number of IHCs in each stack, as determined from maximum-intensity projections of the CtBP2 channel in the x–y plane after increasing image output gain (gamma adjustment). This enhancement reveals IHC nuclei as they stain faintly with the CtBP2 antibody.
Spatial analysis (from high-power images): To assess the spatial organization of IHC afferent synapses and the alignment of IHCs themselves, we defined a habenular–cuticular axis from the y–z projection of each confocal z-stack in custom MATLAB software. The habenula–cuticular axis bisects the subnuclear region of each IHC and runs parallel to unmyelinated afferent fibers, following prior confocal studies in mouse and guinea pig (Yin et al., 2014). An orthogonal (modiolar–pillar) axis was computed, with the coordinate origin defined at the midpoint of the IHC base. IHC alignment was assessed by measuring the silhouette area of all the IHC nuclei projected in the y–z plane. Results from two adjacent z-stacks were averaged at each of the eight cochlear locations.
CNF density analysis (from high-power images): The degree of deinnervation was assessed from maximum-intensity projections of the NF-200 immunostaining in the x–y plane. CNFs in the region around the IHCs include three major components: (1) the lateral olivocochlear efferent fibers coursing along the basal poles of the IHCs, (2) the medial olivocochlear fibers crossing the tunnel of Corti to the OHCs, and (3) the peripheral terminals of afferent fibers projecting to the IHCs. CNFs were quantified using an auto-thresholding algorithm in MATLAB, which measured the total number of suprathreshold pixels in the stack. Results from two adjacent z-stacks were averaged at each of the eight cochlear locations and normalized to the number of IHCs in each stack.
Statistical analysis
Statistical analyses were carried out in MATLAB using two-way ANOVA. Significant effects were followed by post hoc Holm–Šidák corrections for multiple comparisons across frequencies. Multivariable regression analyses with LASSO regularization were carried out in MATLAB, using the lasso function.
Results
CI causes progressive hair cell loss and reduced ABR wave I suprathreshold amplitudes
We performed unilateral CI in adult male CBA/CaJ mice, using the contralateral unimplanted ears as internal controls. All animals recovered uneventfully and survived in good health to their designated endpoints. Cochlear function was assessed immediately prior to tissue harvest at 4 d, 8 weeks, and 16 weeks post-CI. Fibrosis was observed in the 8- and 16-week groups but not at 4 d post-CI or in control ears. In the 8-week group, fibrosis was observed throughout the depth of insertion of the EA, which was estimated to correspond to 32.0 kHz. The fibrosis remains qualitatively unchanged at 16 weeks. No electrode translocations from scala tympani to scala media or scala vestibuli were observed in any specimen. For analysis, the cochlea was divided into apical (<22.6 kHz, low frequency) and basal (≥ 22.6 kHz, high frequency) halves: the apical half lies beyond the EA tip, whereas the basal half is adjacent to the EA (Figs. 1A,B, 2A–4).

Figure 1.
ABR thresholds are elevated and suprathreshold neural responses are progressively attenuated after CI. A, Group means (±SEM) of DPOAE thresholds. For ears without a detectable response at the highest stimulus level (70 dB SPL), thresholds were assigned a value of 80 dB SPL. B, Group means (±SEM) of ABR thresholds. For ears without a recognizable response at the highest stimulus level (90 dB SPL), thresholds were assigned a value of 100 dB SPL. C1–C4, Representative ABR recordings at 11.3 kHz from one animal per group, showing stacked waveforms (tone-pip SPLs indicated on the leftmost axis), wave I amplitude measured as peak-to-trough (red), and visually determined thresholds (bolded). D1–D4, Mean (±SEM) ABR wave I amplitude-level functions for each group in response to tone pips from 8.0 to 22.6 kHz. The slope of each amplitude-level function was calculated by linear regression using suprathreshold wave I amplitudes and is shown as box plots in E1–E4 for 8.0, 11.3, 16.0, and 22.6 kHz, respectively. F1–F4, ABR wave I amplitudes evoked by 90 dB SPL tone pips at 8.0–22.6 kHz, shown as box plots. In all box plots, the median (center line), interquartile range (box), and range (whiskers) are indicated with individual data points overlaid. Group sizes for electrophysiological recordings were control (n = 18 ears, 18 animals), 4 d (n = 6 ears, 6 animals), 8 weeks (n = 6 ears, 6 animals), 16 weeks (n = 6 ears, 6 animals). The key in A applies to A, B, and D1–D4; the key in F3 applies to all box plots. The scale bar in C2 applies to C1–C4. The gray schematics in A and B illustrates the relative position of the EA. Intergroup differences relative to control are indicated by asterisks (*p < 0.05; **p < 0.01; ***p < 0.001 by one-way ANOVA with correction for multiple comparisons).

CI causes progressive hair cell loss from the cochlear base toward the apex. Group sizes for cochlear histology were as follows: control (n = 12 ears from 12 animals; four animals from each group), 4 d (n = 6 ears from 6 animals), 8 weeks (n = 6 ears from 6 animals), 16 weeks (n = 6 ears from 6 animals). The scale bar and key in B4 applies to B1–B4. The legend in A1 applies to A1–A4. For display purposes only, gamma adjustment was applied to images in B1–B4 to enhance the visibility of faint signals; quantitative analyses were performed using the original, unmodified images.
CI-induced threshold shifts began at high frequencies and progressed to lower frequencies over time (Fig. 1A,B). At 4 d post-CI, ABR thresholds were elevated by 25–30 dB in the apical half, while there was a complete loss of response in the basal half (Fig. 1B). Threshold shifts at the lowest test frequency worsened over time; however, the difference was not statistically significant. DPOAE thresholds were significantly elevated at 4 d post-CI and were absent at later time points (Fig. 1A). Control ears showed minimal age-related changes.
CI also caused a marked reduction in ABR wave I suprathreshold amplitudes, beginning at 22.6 kHz—the highest cochlear frequency with a measurable ABR response after implantation—and progressing apically (Fig. 1D,F). At 4 d post-CI, wave I amplitude at 22.6 kHz was reduced by 66% relative to controls (p = 0.0057), with a further 14% reduction observed by 8 weeks (p = 0.0004). The spatial profile of this loss was also evident at 4 d post-CI: amplitude reductions were greatest at 22.6 kHz and tapered toward the apex, with no significant difference at 8 kHz (p = 0.4047). Amplitudes were significantly reduced at all frequencies by 8 weeks post-CI, but there were no further significant declines at 16 weeks. A similar pattern was observed in the slopes of the ABR wave I amplitude-level function (Fig. 1D,E), with differences relative to controls increasing over time and reaching statistical significance across low frequencies by 8 weeks (p = 0.0093).
Control ears showed virtually no IHC or OHC loss over the study period. CI-induced OHC loss was evident by 4 d post-CI at the EA tip and progressed apically (Fig. 2). At 4 d post-CI, substantial OHC loss was observed at the EA tip (50%) and at the base (77%), whereas IHC loss was minimal (15%). OHC loss progressed apically over time and extended beyond the EA tip by 8 weeks post-CI, averaging 30% loss in the apical half. By 16 weeks post-CI, OHC loss at the cochlear apex reached 56%, with near-complete loss (97%) in the basal half. IHC loss followed a similar spatial pattern but progressed more slowly: by 16 weeks post-CI, IHC loss averaged 6% in the apical half and 40% in the basal half of the cochlea.
CI induces IHC shrinkage and CNF loss
To assess the density of CNF terminals in the IHC region, we used anti-neurofilament (NF) immunostaining (Fig. 3). Although the complex arborization of the terminal plexus precluded direct fiber counts, CNF degeneration was evident and quantifiable as changes in NF-positive pixel counts. CNFs at the EA tip (32 kHz) were reduced by 23% as early as 4 d post-CI (p = 0.005). Notably, CNF loss progressed apically over time and extended beyond the EA tip. By 8 weeks post-CI, CNF loss averaged 21.7% in the apical half of the cochlea. This loss plateaued afterward, with only a small additional reduction at 16 weeks.

CI causes IHC atrophy and neural degeneration. Each pair (e.g., A1, B1) shows maximum projections of a confocal z-stack through 10–12 adjacent IHCs from the 8 kHz region, viewed either in the x–y (acquisition) plane (A1–A4) or the y–z (digitally rotated) plane (B1–B4). Gamma adjustment was applied to displayed images to enhance signal visibility; quantitative analyses were performed using the original, unmodified images. Positions of IHC nuclei are indicated by dotted white circles, identified in the green channel by gamma adjustment (data not shown). Yellow dashed lines in B1–B4 denote the modiolar–pillar and habenular–cuticular axes used for spatial analysis of IHC synapses (Fig. 4). In A1 and B1, yellow arrowheads indicate a bundle of medial olivocochlear fibers, yellow arrows indicate the inner spiral bundle, and red arrows indicate a CNF terminating at a synaptic ribbon (open red arrowhead) at the basal pole of an IHC. C, Schematic illustrating CNFs in the IHC region, the staggered positioning of IHC nuclei along the modiolar–pillar axis, and the measurement of “nuclear spread area” used for quantification. The approximate orientations of confocal z-stacks acquired using a 63× objective are indicated. D, Mean (±SEM) CNF density as a function of cochlear frequency, extracted from maximum projections of the NF200 channel in the x–y plane (A1–A4). E, IHC staggering quantified as nuclear spread area measured from y–z projections of confocal z-stacks (B1–B4) and plotted as mean values (±SEM) as a function of cochlear frequency (E). Gray schematics in D and E indicate the relative position of the EA. Values in D and E were normalized to the number of surviving IHCs within the same z-stack. The scale bar in A4 applies to A1–A4 and B1–B4. Group sizes are the same as in Figure 2. Symbol keys in E also apply to D. Intergroup differences relative to control are indicated by asterisks (*p < 0.05; **p < 0.01; ***p < 0.001 by two-way ANOVA with correction for multiple comparisons).
In normal adult mouse cochleae, adjacent IHC nuclei occupy staggered positions along the hair cell row, because the IHCs are flask-shaped, i.e., wider in the nuclear region than at the cuticular plates (Yin et al., 2014), where they are firmly held by tight junctions in the reticular lamina (Gulley and Reese, 1976). This staggering produces an expansion of the IHC nuclear profiles in maximum-intensity confocal projections of the y–z plane (Fig. 3B1). Nuclear staggering was quantified by measuring the silhouette area normalized to the number of IHCs in each image stack (Fig. 3B1–B4). The magnitude of IHC staggering peaked near 22.6 kHz, reflecting the combined effects of increasing IHC packing density toward the apex and enlargement of individual IHCs toward the mid-frequency locations. In implanted ears, loss of nuclear staggering became significant by 16 weeks post-CI and was most pronounced near 22.6 kHz (Fig. 3E; 28% reduction; p = 0.0013). Because measurements were normalized to IHC cell number, this reduction is attributable primarily to IHC shrinkage rather than to cell loss.
IHC staggering also contributes to the elongated shape of the synaptic “cloud”—defined as the spatial envelope containing synapses—along the modiolar–pillar axis in control ears (Fig. 4, first column). In CI ears, the synaptic cloud becomes more compact, reflecting both the loss of staggered IHC alignment and an overall reduction in synapse number (Fig. 4, rightmost column).

CI induces loss of IHC afferent synapses
In the normal cochlea, each CNF typically forms a single synaptic contact with an IHC, and each IHC is contacted by multiple CNFs that differ in spontaneous rate (SR) and threshold (Liberman and Kiang, 1978; Liberman, 1980). These fibers are spatially polarized around the IHC circumference, with low-SR fibers terminating preferentially on the modiolar side of the IHC and high-SR fibers on the pillar side. High-threshold, low-SR fibers are important for hearing in noisy environments (Costalupes et al., 1984) and are more vulnerable to aging and noise exposure (Schmiedt et al., 1996; Furman et al., 2013; Liberman et al., 2015).
We quantified synapse counts from confocal z-stacks acquired across eight cochlear regions spaced at half-octave intervals. In control ears, synapse counts peaked in mid-cochlear regions (∼18/IHC; Fig. 5A) and declined toward the apex and base (∼9/IHC; Fig. 5A), consistent with prior studies (Kujawa and Liberman, 2009). In CI ears, synapse loss was evident as early as 4 d post-CI at 32 kHz, with synapse counts reduced to ∼11 synapses/IHC, compared with 16 synapses/IHC in control ears (Fig. 5A). By 8 weeks, the loss had spread both basally and apically, including regions beyond the electrode tip. By 16 weeks, ∼61% of synapses were lost across the cochlea (p < 0.001).

CI induces rapid loss of IHC synapses across the cochlea, with preferential loss of modiolar synapses at early stages. A, Mean (±SEM) synapse count per IHC along the cochlear spiral. The vertical dashed line indicates the boundary separating the cochlear apex from the base. B, Percentage survival of synapses on the pillar versus modiolar side. To reduce the number of plotted symbols, the cochlea was divided into three frequency segments (cutoffs at 8.0 and 22.6 kHz, inclusive), and a single symbol was used to represent data within each segment. Each data point represents the number of synapses on the pillar or modiolar side, averaged across two adjacent confocal stacks at a given cochlear frequency and normalized to the number of IHCs in each stack and to control values. C, D, Mean (±SEM) synapse count per IHC on the modiolar (C) and pillar (D) sides along the cochlear spiral. E, Mean (±SEM) percentage of synapses on the pillar side along the cochlear spiral. Gray schematics in A, C, and D indicate the relative position of the EA. The key in C applies to A, C, D, and E. Group sizes are the same as in Figure 2. Intergroup differences relative to control are indicated by asterisks and color-coded to match the corresponding symbols (*p < 0.05; **p < 0.01; ***p < 0.001 by two-way ANOVA with correction for multiple comparisons).
To assess whether CI preferentially affected high- versus low-SR fibers, synapses were classified as pillar versus modiolar side based on their position relative to the hair cell axis within each z-stack (Fig. 3B1–B4); this analysis is summarized in Figure 4. In control ears, synaptic density was consistently greater on the modiolar side across the cochlea, with quantitative analysis showing that ∼34% of synapses occupied the pillar half (Fig. 5E), consistent with prior confocal and ultrastructural studies (Liberman, 1980; Yin et al., 2014). At 4 d after CI, this spatial gradient was disrupted, with a preferential reduction of modiolar-side synapses reflected as a consistent, though not statistically significant, increase in the proportion of pillar side synapses across cochlear locations (Fig. 5E). Interpretation of the 8 and 16 week data is complicated by the progressive loss of IHC staggering, which became significant by 8 weeks post-CI. In control and 4 d ears, IHC staggering introduces some misassignment when a single plane is used to divide all IHCs within a z-stack into pillar and modiolar halves. Although the loss of staggering at 8 and 16 weeks makes this geometric division more accurate, the resulting spatial gradients are not directly comparable across groups.
Predicting synapse counts from ABR metrics
To compare CI-induced degeneration across cochlear structures, histological measurements were plotted against one another (Fig. 6). CNF density was strongly correlated with synapse count in both the apical and basal halves of the cochlea (Fig. 6D). Survival of IHCs and OHCs was largely independent of changes in other cochlear structures, particularly in the apical half (Fig. 6B,C,E,F). Notably, synapse loss exceeded both CNF loss and IHC loss in CI ears (Fig. 6F), indicating that many surviving IHCs lacked afferent synaptic contacts and that many CNF terminals survive despite loss of synaptic specialization with IHCs.

Figure 6.
Pairwise analysis reveals a strong correlation between synapse count and CNF density, independent of hair cell survival. Each point in the scatterplots represents the mean value of the indicated metric, binned across cochlear regions as indicated in the key: thus, each case contributes eight points to each graph. Least-squares regressions were computed separately for the apical and basal halves of the cochlea (solid and dashed lines, respectively). Adjusted r2 values for the apical half (ah) and basal half (bh) are shown in each subplot. All regression analyses yielded p values <0.0001. Group sizes are the same as in Figure 2. Histological data were normalized to the 95th percentile of the group distribution.
To determine whether CI-induced primary neural degeneration could be predicted using noninvasive electrophysiological measures, we examined three ABR-derived predictors: ABR threshold, the slope of the wave I amplitude-level function, and suprathreshold amplitude measured at 5 dB above threshold. We restricted this analysis to ABR measurements obtained at half-octave intervals within the 8–22.6 kHz frequency range and synapse counts corresponding to this tonotopic region. Among individual predictors, synapse count was most strongly associated with ABR threshold (adjusted r2 = 0.143; p < 0.001; Fig. 7B), followed closely by the slope of the wave I amplitude-level function (adjusted r2 = 0.140; p < 0.001; Fig. 7C). However, collinearity among electrophysiological measures complicated interpretation (Fig. 7D–F). We therefore applied multivariable linear regression with LASSO regularization (Yang and Zou, 2015), which penalizes correlated predictors and yields a sparse model in which each predictor's relative contribution is reflected by the magnitude of its coefficient.

Figure 7.
CI-induced synapse loss can be predicted by the slope of the ABR wave I amplitude-level function. A–C, Regression analyses of three electrophysiological predictors of synapse survival. D–F, Pairwise correlations among these predictors. Synapse data were binned and averaged across cochlear locations corresponding to each test frequency (8.0–22.6 kHz); thus, each case contributes four points to each graph. Synapse values were normalized to the 95th percentile of the group distribution. Electrophysiological predictors were derived from ABR waveforms. Wave I amplitude measured at 5 dB above threshold was selected to minimize confounding by threshold elevation; this value was set to zero when thresholds reached 90 dB SPL or when ears were nonresponsive (assigned 100 dB SPL). Electrophysiological measures were normalized to the 95th percentile of the group distribution. G, H, Multivariable LASSO regression models used to derive the best weights (coefficients) of electrophysiological metrics for predicting synapse survival, either including (G) or excluding (H) control ears. Differences in coefficient polarity (G2–H2) arise because synapse counts are expressed as survival and are positively correlated with the ABR wave I amplitude–growth slope and suprathreshold wave I amplitude, whereas ABR threshold is expressed as a loss and is negatively correlated with synapse survival. Implantation status was included as a binary predictor (1 = CI ear) and is negatively correlated with synapse survival. Two regression analyses were conducted separately using all data points from 8.0–22.6 kHz of the control and CI ears (G) or only the CI ears (H). Group sizes are the same as in Figure 2. The key in G1 applies to panels A–F, G1, and H1.
In the final LASSO model, the slope of the wave I amplitude-level function emerged as the strongest contributor, whereas ABR threshold and suprathreshold amplitude at 5 dB above threshold contributed less (Fig. 7G). Adding implantation status further improved model performance, likely reflecting CI-induced cochlear changes not captured in the model, such as hair cell loss, scala tympani fibrosis, or chronic conductive deficits. A LASSO regression model restricted to CI ears alone yielded similar results (Fig. 7H), with the slope of the wave I amplitude-level function remaining the dominant predictor. Together, these findings indicate that synapse survival in CI ears with preserved low-frequency hearing is best predicted by the slope of the ABR wave I amplitude-level function.
Discussion
CI-induced primary neural degeneration
CI induces degeneration of afferent synapses and CNFs that progresses spatially and temporally (Figs. 3D, 5). Early post-CI, synapse loss is localized near the EA tip but progressively spreads apically to cochlear regions beyond the array. At the cochlear apex, synapse loss outpaced OHC loss (Fig. 8A), a pattern that predicts neural dysfunction despite preserved OHC function. Consistent with this interpretation, audiometric data from a subset of HPCI patients show delayed elevation of low-frequency acoustic thresholds, characterized by loss of the auditory nerve neurophonic (i.e., neural responses) while cochlear microphonics (i.e., OHC responses) persist (Tejani et al., 2021). Within this apical region, in our mouse model, synapse loss also greatly exceeds IHC loss, occurring more than sevenfold faster (0.5 vs 0.07% per day; Fig. 8C,D), and CNF loss similarly exceeds IHC loss (p < 0.0001 by ANOVA; Fig. 8D). Together, these results demonstrate extensive survival of deafferented IHCs, i.e., primary neural degeneration (Fig. 8A). This pattern indicates that CI at the base can induce neural degeneration independent of, and preceding, overt hair cell loss at the cochlear apex.

P values for the difference in regression slopes between predictors are indicated graphically in D. The key in A applies to A and B. Group sizes are the same as in Figure 2.
Although primary neural degeneration may have limited effects on audiometric thresholds if diffusely distributed (Schuknecht and Woellner, 1955), even partial neural loss may degrade speech perception, particularly in noisy environments. Indeed, deficits in complex listening conditions have been linked to relatively smaller neural responsiveness in ears with robust hair cell responses (Liberman et al., 2016).
This CI-induced primary cochlear neural degeneration has several clinical implications. First, the rate of residual hearing loss following HPCI may partly depend on the number of functional CNFs present prior to implantation (Wu et al., 2018, 2020), such that ears with more robust innervation may take longer to manifest threshold elevation. Consistent with this view, age and prior noise exposure—both risk factors for loss of residual acoustic hearing after HPCI (Kopelovich et al., 2014; Jensen et al., 2022)—are associated with disproportionate neural loss relative to hair cell loss (Kujawa and Liberman, 2009; Wu et al., 2018, 2021). Second, the current definition of HPCI, which relies on audiometric threshold measures, likely substantially underestimates CI-induced neural degeneration. Accordingly, strategies aimed at preserving synapses and CNFs post-CI may provide additional benefits for hearing outcomes, including improved speech performance in challenging environments. In this regard, ECochG measurements may provide sensitive indicators of synaptic, neural, and hair cell degeneration after CI and may prove useful in clinical studies seeking to preserve hearing in CI patients.
CI-induced hair cell degeneration
In addition to primary neural degeneration, CI induces progressive hair cell loss with a distinct spatial and temporal profile: hair cell loss starts at the EA tip and the base of the cochlea, worsens over time, and extends to cochlear regions apical to the EA tip (Fig. 2). IHCs are more robust, and their degeneration is slower than that of OHCs.
The spatial pattern observed at early stages likely reflects the combined effect of the apical–basal gradient of scala tympani volume and the intrinsic vulnerability of the cochlear base. As the scala tympani tapers toward the apex, the EA occupies a larger fraction of the lumen (Reiss et al., 2022). Consistent with this gradient, OHC survival improved at 45.2 kHz (Fig. 2A2), before declining again toward the base, a region known to be particularly susceptible to cochlear insults. These spatial trends were observed in the absence of electrode translocation, indicating that gross disruption of the basilar membrane is not required. Instead, the pattern likely reflects multiple, nonmutually exclusive mechanisms related to electrode placement within a confined scala tympani, damage to the lateral wall, and inflammatory responses to implanted biomaterials (Ishiyama et al., 2019; Rahman et al., 2022; Reiss et al., 2022).
While it is not feasible to obtain a similar time-series study in humans, results of several human studies provide evidence for hair cell degeneration following CI. A recent human temporal bone study of an HPCI patient demonstrated substantial degeneration of both IHCs and OHCs in the implanted cochlea compared with the unimplanted ear (O’Malley et al., 2024). In another study, seven HPCI patients were followed for up to 60 months post-CI and exhibited gradual loss of the cochlear microphonic component in all subjects (Tejani et al., 2021). Histology was not available in this study. If the temporal pattern observed here extends to humans, these findings are consistent with CI accelerating age-related hair cell degeneration in the implanted ear, reflected by progressive reduction in cochlear microphonic function (Pappa et al., 2019).
Comparison to other studies: problems and pitfalls
The spatiotemporal patterns described here also help explain discrepancies across prior studies. CI-induced hair cell loss has been studied in both animal models (Eshraghi et al., 2013; O’Leary et al., 2013; Tanaka et al., 2014; Reiss et al., 2015; Swiderski et al., 2020; Colesa et al., 2021; Claussen et al., 2022) and in humans (Fayad et al., 1991; Quesnel et al., 2016; Geerardyn et al., 2023; O’Malley et al., 2024), with inconsistent conclusions ranging from minimal loss to losses approaching 80%, highlighting substantial challenges in cross-study comparisons. Although differences in study design, surgical technique, and implant design influence the results, we propose that much of the discrepancy across studies may stem from the interaction between the histological approach used to assess hair cell survival and the spatiotemporal pattern of CI-induced hair cell degeneration.
One major methodological source of variability across animal studies is the histological approach used to assess hair cell survival, i.e., serial sectioning versus whole-mount preparations. When hair cell loss is widespread, estimates of hair cell survival derived from these approaches are often comparable. In contrast, when hair cell loss is spotty—as in the complex spatiotemporal pattern seen in CI ears—accurate characterization requires dense spatial sampling offered by whole-mount preparations. Studies relying on sparse sampling, such as a limited number of midmodiolar sections (Leake-Jones and Rebscher, 1983; Kang et al., 2010; Pfingst et al., 2011; O’Leary et al., 2013; Tanaka et al., 2014; Colesa et al., 2021), may yield highly variable estimates of hair cell survival, whereas whole-mount studies sampling larger cochlear regions (Eshraghi et al., 2013; Nieratschker et al., 2024) consistently report greater CI-induced hair cell loss.
These methodological limitations also affect human temporal bone studies, most of which report minimal hair cell loss following CI (Quesnel et al., 2016; Knoll et al., 2022; Geerardyn et al., 2023, 2024). These studies typically rely on serially sectioned and H&E-stained material (Merchant and Nadol, 2010) and use a binarized scoring approach (Suzuka and Schuknecht, 1988; Schuknecht and Gacek, 1993; Quesnel et al., 2016), in which hair cells are only scored as present or absent in a tissue section despite the fact that each view of the organ of Corti contains at least 3–4 hair cells in each row. This approach substantially underestimates hair cell loss when degeneration is incomplete (Wu et al., 2019). A recent reevaluation of an HPCI case (O’Malley et al., 2024) using high-resolution differential interference contrast microscopy and assessing fractional hair cell survival revealed extensive OHC loss that was not detected by binary analyses of the same specimen (Quesnel et al., 2016).
In addition to these methodological considerations, several limitations of the present study should be acknowledged. The relative size of the EA within the mouse scala tympani is substantially larger than in the human cochlea (Reiss et al., 2022), likely exaggerating implantation-related trauma and associated histopathological changes. Furthermore, the absence of electrical stimulation in the CI cochleae may lead to underestimation of CI-induced primary neural degeneration, as previous studies suggest that electrical stimulation can increase synapse loss (Li et al., 2020). The mice used here had normal hearing at implantation, whereas most human CI recipients exhibit preexisting high-frequency hearing loss due to genetic factors, aging, noise exposure, ototoxicity, and/or other insults. How CI-induced cochlear degeneration interacts with premorbid cochlear pathology therefore remains unclear.

Viewpoints
Mammalian Brains Seen through the Lens of Evolution
S. Murray Sherman and W. Martin Usrey
Journal of Neuroscience 3 June 2026, 46 (22) e0217262026; https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.0217-26.2026
S. Murray Sherman
1Department of Neurobiology, University of Chicago, Chicago, Illinois 60637
Find this author on Google Scholar
Find this author on PubMed
Search for this author on this site
ORCID record for S. Murray Sherman
W. Martin Usrey
2Center for Neuroscience, University of California, Davis, California 95618
Find this author on Google Scholar
Find this author on PubMed
Search for this author on this site
ORCID record for W. Martin Usrey
Article
Figures & Data
Info & Metrics
eLetters
PDF
Abstract
This Viewpoint argues that understanding how the brain controls behavior requires an explicitly evolutionary framework. The mammalian brain did not emerge through the replacement of earlier circuits with perfect alternatives but rather through the elaboration of existing circuits along with the addition of new ones, yielding a hierarchical architecture in which many ancient spinal and brainstem circuits remain functionally essential. In this context, cortex does not directly control behavior but exerts its influence via layer 5 projections to evolutionarily older subcortical motor centers. This view challenges the prevailing corticocentric bias in neuroscience, which often treats cortex as a largely self-contained computational system. We propose that key functions such as attention and efference copy are best understood within this layered organization. Attention may reflect competitive filtering of corticofugal outputs at subcortical bottlenecks, while efference copies arise naturally from branching motor pathways distributed across hierarchical levels that reflect evolutionary history. Crucially, these principles expose important limitations in current computational models, which typically omit subcortical circuitry and treat motor output as a terminal stage of processing. An evolutionary perspective instead demands models that integrate cortex with spinal, brainstem, and midbrain systems as interacting components of a unified sensorimotor hierarchy. Incorporating these constraints will be essential for developing biologically grounded theories of brain function.

Significance Statement
Understanding how the brain controls behavior remains a central goal of neuroscience, yet most approaches emphasize cortical processing in isolation. This article highlights the importance of an evolutionary framework, showing that mammalian brains are organized as layered systems in which newer cortical circuits interact with older subcortical motor networks. We argue that cortex influences behavior primarily through these evolutionarily conserved pathways, which continue to operate independently and cooperatively. This perspective challenges corticocentric models and has important implications for interpreting attention, efference copy, and corticothalamic communication. Incorporating subcortical circuitry into experimental and computational approaches will be essential for developing more complete and biologically grounded models of brain function.
An understanding of the human brain ultimately requires an appreciation of how evolution produced it (Kaas, 2013; Cisek and Hayden, 2022). The cerebral cortex represents one of the most prominent evolutionary developments in mammalian brains and has therefore become a central focus of neuroscience investigation. Implicitly or explicitly, the goal is to understand how the brain, and especially the cortex, controls behavior. (Here we use “cortex” throughout to mean “neocortex.”) However, the vast majority of work is limited to describing the functioning of an isolated cortex—individual areas or communication among them—which, while clearly valuable, fails to address how cortex actually controls behavior. We argue that the key to this requires an understanding of the role of evolution.
What follows is a discussion of how the process of evolution has plausibly produced mammalian brains (e.g., human) from primitive vertebrate ancestors (e.g., lamprey). We also emphasize various limitations and complications that have arisen as a result. An important proviso to keep in mind is that studies of evolution generally depend on fossil evidence, but since nervous systems do not fossilize, we are left with frustratingly indirect clues as to the evolutionary processes that result in our brains. Thus, we offer speculations that are meant more as suggestions for further consideration than as clearly accepted dogma, and we suggest important questions that arise from this perspective.
Evolution Is Messy
As new environmental challenges are met, evolution does not always result in an ideal solution, and the solution is often rather messy. A key feature of brain evolution is that any existing cell and circuit properties that remain useful are maintained, and evolution often responds to environmental challenges by adding new circuits, not replacing older, still useful ones. Thus, evolution works by modification and addition, not major redesign. Instead of replacing older circuits with more efficient ones, yielding a neater, less complex brain, evolution retains useful older circuits through which the brain operates.
This is the context in which cortical functioning must be seen. The evolution of cortex did not result in a co-evolution of a motor plant through which cortex could directly act. That is, with very rare exceptions, layer 5 projections do not directly innervate motoneurons to carry out directed behaviors. Instead, the route by which cortex can control behavior is via layer 5 projections to earlier evolved motor centers in the brainstem and spinal cord (Usrey and Sherman, 2019). This does not mean the cortex in mammals must rely on circuits evolved with no further enhancements in non-mammalian vertebrates, because these circuits continue to evolve to become more efficient and flexible (Northcutt, 2002). In this way, cortex need not “reinvent the wheel” but can plug into a variety of subcortical circuits to control behavior. This evolutionary perspective is a sine qua non for a full understanding of cortical functioning. This evolutionary process of keeping useful circuitry and adding on newer circuits to provide more flexible behavior is messy, but an important result is that successful subcortical circuits continue to function in mammalian brains endowed with cortex. This subcortical functioning independent of cortex is frequently overlooked in a corticocentric field.
Figure 1 illustrates some of these points by comparing non-mammalian vertebrate brains (Fig. 1A,B) with the human brain (Fig. 1C). The lamprey is a primitive, living vertebrate and thus serves as a model for the brains of our early vertebrate ancestors. The comparisons of Figure 1 offer some possible insights into the evolutionary bridge leading to the appearance of the human brain.

Figure 1.
Schematic view of evolutionary stages of vertebrate central nervous system to human. A, Primitive vertebrate (e.g., lamprey). Its central nervous system controls behavior largely via vestibulospinal and reticulospinal tracts to control central pattern generators (CPGs; for swimming behavior, etc.). Redrawn from Grillner (2003); Stuart (2007); Suryanarayana et al. (2017). B, More advanced vertebrate (e.g., birds and most reptiles). The main supraspinal pathways remain, but the tectospinal and rubrospinal tracts as well some additional brainstem circuitry are added through evolution. C, Human central nervous system. Here, most of the older supraspinal circuits exist, and evolution has added more brainstem circuits and cortical control of many lower motor centers. Note that cortex must operate via layer 5 projections to centers evolved in A and B and that many of the circuits that evolved in A and B remain functional in C.
Much of the neuronal control of early vertebrates was based on central pattern generators (CPGs). These are intrinsically organized spinal cord circuits capable of producing rhythmic motor patterns, such as alternating left–right contractions for swimming, without requiring patterned sensory input or descending commands. Studies of the lamprey demonstrate that isolated spinal cords can generate fictive swimming, supporting the idea that such circuitry is evolutionarily ancient and was already present near the base of vertebrate phylogeny (Grillner and Wallén, 1985; Grillner, 2006). Furthermore, comparative and developmental studies suggest that the core genetic and cellular building blocks of these CPGs are conserved across vertebrates (Goulding, 2009; Kiehn, 2016). These spinal circuits appear to operate in lampreys with some supraspinal control from the brainstem (Fig. 1A). Such circuits evolved with further additions in more advanced vertebrates (Fig. 1B), and we suggest that the supraspinal circuits illustrated in Figure 1A,B are homologous to those in the human brain (Fig. 1C).
We repeat: brain evolution mostly involves meeting environmental challenges not by replacing circuits with better models but rather by keeping useful circuits and adding newer ones that work with remaining, older circuitry to provide more flexible and efficient responses to the environment. The human brain evolved from those of our primitive vertebrate ancestors, which we assume are well modeled by the lamprey brain. Thus, one can imagine that this process is suggested by proceeding from the model of Figure 1A through that of Figure 1B to that of Figure 1C. Each step involves the addition of supraspinal circuitry to control spinal circuits and, ultimately, motoneuron outputs. One can imagine a similar process for control of movements of the head, eyes, etc. that are mostly supraspinal and not considered further here. This process of evolution is meant in the most general, abstract terms as follows:
The homologs of the vestibulospinal and reticulospinal tracts remain, but additional tracts evolved: the tectospinal and rubrospinal. (Other tracts from brainstem to spinal cord also exist, but for simplicity to make our general point about the complex result of brain evolution, we ignore these.) These appear as a hierarchy reflecting evolutionary history. Each offers an upgrade of more flexible and effective control of behavior.
The acme seen in non-mammalian vertebrates such as birds and reptiles is the tectospinal pathway, which reflects the highest level of sensorimotor integration in midbrain circuitry.
Nonetheless, all of these supraspinal tracts still operate in humans, which requires coordination among them to avoid a chaotic situation in neuronal control of behavior. This seems messier than simply replacing earlier supraspinal tract with newer, better models, but as noted, this is not the way of evolution.
The last evolutionary addition to control behavior is the appearance of the cortex.
However, in order for the cortex to control behavior, it must operate through evolutionary older structures in the brainstem and spinal cord, mostly via the supraspinal tracts shown in Figure 1C, rather than by directly controlling motoneurons. Only in rare cases do corticofugal axons enter the spinal cord and effectively only in Old World monkeys and apes (including humans) is there a direct input from primary motor cortex to spinal motoneurons (Strick et al., 2021).
Thus, as we have noted above, the evolution of cortex did not result in a co-evolution of a motor plant through which cortex could directly act. Instead, its only route to control behavior is via layer 5 projections to earlier evolved motor centers in the brainstem and spinal cord. As noted, these older structures can be seen as a hierarchy representing evolutionary history: central pattern generators in the spinal cord, various supraspinal centers that operate through these spinal circuits, and corticofugal pathways that operate chiefly through these supraspinal centers. As a result, to effect complicated motor actions, cortex does not have to reproduce all the necessary circuitry but instead can operate through subcortical centers through which the appropriate commands can be produced. As suggested by Figure 1C, every cortical area so far studied has a layer 5 projection that innervates subcortical motor centers (Prasad et al., 2020; Sherman and Usrey, 2024).
This, then, is the suggested model for the evolution of our brains and, in particular, the functional organization by which cortex controls behavior. It is complicated and seems to require that the different levels of motor control function cooperatively. We note below several issues raised by this arrangement for cortical control of behavior.
Corticocentricity Is Misleading
The study of neuroscience operates chiefly through a corticocentric bias: the assertion that cortex is the principal and often sole site of sensory processing, motor control, and sensorimotor integration. This bias is evident in influential textbooks (Squire et al., 2014; Kandel et al., 2021; Augustine et al., 2023) as well as other books (Mountcastle, 1998; Dehaene, 2014; Gazzaniga, 2019). Clearly, cortex is essential for many important functions, such as flexible perception, abstraction, and motor learning, but much of our behavior is based on subcortical circuits that operate in parallel and often independently of cortex (Parvizi, 2009; Chin et al., 2023).
Subcortical control of motor tasks
Much of our everyday behavior is accomplished without active cortical involvement. Examples of such behavior include normal breathing, walking along a familiar path, chewing food, and many others. Indeed, classic decortication studies in cats and rodents show that locomotion, grooming, feeding, and defensive behaviors persist after extensive cortical removal, implicating brainstem circuits in generating coherent action patterns (Sherrington, 1906; Bard, 1928); see also (Chambers et al., 1966; McCouch et al., 1966; Musienko et al., 2012; Storbeck et al., 2014).
Whereas cortex is generally needed to learn new motor skills, many of these skills, once learned, can be executed after motor cortical disruption, indicating that subcortical networks (basal ganglia, cerebellum, brainstem, spinal circuits) can sustain automatized performance (Graybiel, 2008; Kawai et al., 2015; Mizes et al., 2024). Furthermore, the superior colliculus is heavily involved in the creation of saccades, and evidence exists that some such eye movements can be initiated by the colliculus independently of cortex (Hafed et al., 2021). It is also notable that some behavior, such as fear conditioning (LeDoux, 2000; Maren and Quirk, 2004) plus sound and visual detection (Sherman, 1974; Lee et al., 2024) can be initially learned independently of cortex.
Importance of the superior colliculus
As noted above, the superior colliculus has evolved to become the most effective sensorimotor structure in non-mammalian vertebrates and remains an important sensorimotor structure in mammals. Briefly, the superior colliculus is a layered structure. The dorsal layers are sensory, starting most dorsally with vision, below these are layers representing multimodal sensory integration, and most ventrally are the majority of collicular projections, including motor outputs. Further details of the organization of the superior colliculus can be found elsewhere (May, 2006; Cang et al., 2018; Basso et al., 2021; Wheatcroft et al., 2022; Hoy and Farrow, 2025).
Considerable evidence has accumulated that the mammalian superior colliculus often operates independently of cortex to control certain behaviors. The Sprague effect (Sprague, 1966) is an illuminating example of such functioning (see also Sherman, 1974, 1977; Ciaramitaro et al., 1997b; Zénon and Krauzlis, 2012; Krauzlis et al., 2013, 2018; Basso and May, 2017; Cang et al., 2018; Bogadhi et al., 2021). Sprague showed that lesions of visual cortex in cats lead to expected blindness in the affected areas of visual field but that much vision can be restored independent of cortex and supported by the superior colliculus with appropriate subsequent midbrain surgery. Basically, the cortical lesion removes excitatory input to the superior colliculus, leading to an unbalanced dominance of inhibition of collicular neurons, effectively silencing these cells. By additional surgery that removes inhibitory input to collicular cells by lesioning such input from the substantia nigra, balance is restored, and the superior colliculus can now function to support various aspects of behavior (Ciaramitaro et al., 1997a,b). Evidence for the Sprague effect has been extended to humans (Weddell, 2004), and such restoration of sensory function following cortical ablation has also been extended to the auditory system (Lomber et al., 2007). Clearly, the superior colliculus can support sensorimotor functions independent of cortex, and this serves as an example noted above of circuitry that evolved in our vertebrate ancestors and remains functional in mammalian brains even after cortex evolved.
Cortex operates through older subcortical motor centers
Taken together, these findings argue against a cortex-exclusive view and instead support a distributed, hierarchical architecture in which subcortical circuits are not passive relays but essential computational nodes in sensory, motor, and sensorimotor processing. Cortex does often operate through these systems and indeed is effectively the only way that cortex can influence behavior.
As noted above, cortex affects behavior via layer 5 projections to these subcortical centers. Without these outputs, cortex would be pretty useless (Fig. 1C). Of particular interest is the observation that every cortical area so far studied has such a layer 5 output, and thus every cortical area is positioned to influence behavior. By this arrangement, cortex can select highly evolved motor motifs by choosing which combination of subcortical structures to activate. This is much like a conductor determining which instruments and melodies the orchestra should play.
Figure 1C illustrates the point that, to understand cortical functioning writ large, it is necessary but insufficient to study the function of cortical areas without regard to how cortex communicates with subcortical partners. This aspect of cortical functioning has not been sufficiently studied. We must place more emphasis on understanding the interplay between cortex and the rest of the neuroaxis, especially as regards layer 5 corticofugal projections.
Evolution and Attention
The vast majority of studies of attention in mammals focus on effects in cortex and emphasize the improved circuit functioning of involved areas, such as regions of visual cortex related to visual field locations in which attention is directed. Such effects among neurons include larger evoked visual responses (Maunsell and Treue, 2006; Lee and Maunsell, 2010; Mineault et al., 2016; Suzuki et al., 2019), reduced noise correlations (Cohen and Maunsell, 2009), synchronization among neurons (Womelsdorf et al., 2006), and enhanced thalamocortical synaptic efficacy (Briggs et al., 2013). Attention has also been reported to create coherent, rhythmic neuronal firing across cortical areas (Fries, 2005; Chalk et al., 2010; Saalmann et al., 2012; Miller and Buschman, 2013; Suzuki et al., 2019).
According to current dogma, such attentional mechanisms evolved to ensure that brain mechanisms focus on environmental events that are most critical for survival. For example, a mouse traveling through underbrush might emphasize visual search of the sky to detect hawks overhead. This selective attention, however, comes at a price: such concentration on visual stimuli may cause the mouse to miss auditory cues signaling a stalking fox. Even within vision, attention has trade-offs—focusing on the upper visual field to track hawks may reduce sensitivity to predators in the lower visual field. Attention can thus be viewed as a zero-sum game, where devoting resources to one set of neural circuits necessarily reduces resources available to others. This raises a fundamental question: given the vast computational capacity of cortical circuits, why cannot all areas of cortex function in an attentive mode simultaneously, ensuring maximum sensitivity to all sensory inputs? We believe that an evolutionary perspective offers a plausible answer to this question.
Corticofugal motor commands operate through a subcortical bottleneck
Figure 1C shows that layer 5 inputs from many and sometimes all cortical areas converge onto subcortical sites. The superior colliculus provides a good example of this for two reasons noted above: (1) it is the most highly evolved subcortical sensorimotor command center and thus offers cortex the most flexible and efficient route through which to affect behavior; and (2) every cortical area so far studied has a layer 5 projection to the superior colliculus.
This sets up the superior colliculus as a bottleneck through which cortex must operate. That is, projections from the superior colliculus to control behavior, such as via the tectospinal tract and analogous output to cranial motor centers, are potentially activated via layer 5 projections, which exist from most or all cortical areas; if all of these cortical areas vied for control of these collicular outputs, chaos would ensue (Fig. 2A). Of course, as noted, the colliculus can act independently of cortex (Fig. 2B). However, when cortex does control behavior through the colliculus, only the appropriate area(s) may do so. Thus, when cortical attention is directed through vision, visual cortical areas have effectively exclusive access (Fig. 2C); when directed through audition, auditory areas dominate (Fig. 2D), etc. The neuronal substrates for this filtering of cortical inputs can only be imagined for now (see below).

Figure 2.
Diagrams showing aspects of corticofugal control of behavior related to attention via layer 5 projections to superior colliculus (Sherman and Usrey, 2021). Note convergence of inputs to the superior colliculus from different cortical areas. A, If all cortical areas simultaneously acted to control behavior, chaos would ensue, and the tectospinal tract would not carry proper commands. Filtering is needed to ensure that the appropriate cortical areas activate the tectospinal tract (C, D). B, For behaviors controlled subcortically and independent of cortex, no cortical area activates the tectospinal tract. C, For attention dominated by vision, only visual cortex activates the tectospinal tract. D, For attention dominated by audition, only auditory cortex activates the tectospinal tract.
This is not to say that attention-mediated, enhanced layer 5 activity mandates a motor response, as we can certainly attend to elements in our environment without evoking motor behavior. For instance, while sitting quietly and attending to different instruments in a symphony, there is little or no cortical control of behavior; and any behavior ongoing at the time, such as breathing, etc., is controlled by subcortical circuits independently of cortex (Fig. 2B).
The subject of attention has received considerable study in neuroscience research. Most of this work has been devoted to understanding the neuronal basis of attention within cortical circuitry. We suggest that an important, heretofore unexplored, aspect of neural substrates of attention is the filtering that occurs in determining which among multiple afferents carrying motor commands to subcortical motor centers pass through the filtering to control outputs of these centers. Perhaps even attention itself may be defined and detected by the presence of such neuronal filtering.
Attention is not just cortical
A clear example of corticocentricity is that virtually all studies of attention focus exclusively on cortical (or thalamocortical) contributions. The evolution of mammalian brains has created a more complex picture regarding the neuronal bases of attention. We argue that attention serves to ensure that the most relevant brain circuits control behavior, but this principle is not unique to mammals with a well-developed cortex. For instance, non-mammalian vertebrates, lacking cortex, face the same problem of selecting the appropriate motor commands. The midbrain serves as an example of this: evolution has produced the most complex and flexible circuits for subcortical behavioral control in the midbrain, which in turn act through older pathways, such as bulbospinal and spinal circuits. As in mammals, these systems required a mechanism to prevent conflicting outputs; without such filtering, multiple inputs, such as different sensory stimuli, targeting the midbrain centers could compete for control and produce chaos. Thus, even in non-mammalian vertebrates, attentional-like processes would have been necessary to regulate which midbrain centers dominated behavior.
We have asserted that, as newer brain circuits evolve, it is generally the case that older, useful ones are not discarded but continue to function. This includes subcortical circuits that serve independently of cortex to initiate appropriate behavioral responses to environmental challenges. For instance, imagine we are walking through the woods on an unfamiliar path with a friend with whom we are carrying on a conversation. We might consciously attend to the conversation (the cortical part), but subcortical centers are “attending” to the path to make sure we do not trip over a root (e.g., we make saccades 3–5 times per second that we do not consciously evoke in order to scan the environment). This may be viewed as an example of attention processes subcortically. We thus conclude that attention is not a single, peremptory cortical process but involves cortical and subcortical processes that work together in harmony to control appropriate behavior. How this is accomplished needs to be understood and at least appreciated as an important question to resolve.
Speculations on the filtering circuitry underlying attention
If our suggestion that attention relies on filtering of inputs to subcortical motor centers, the question arises: what is the neuronal substrate for such filtering? Regarding, for example, the filtering of cortical layer 5 inputs to the superior colliculus, the question centers on whether this is done in cortex, to silence the layer 5 outputs, or at the collicular target, to block the synaptic effects of these outputs. Both possibilities need to be tested experimentally. The rationale for suggesting the filtering is done in cortex to inhibit inappropriate layer 5 projections seems clear, but the idea of the filtering being done subcortically requires further explanation. We have argued that this sort of subcortical filtering must have evolved before cortex in non-mammalian vertebrates, since such filtering seems likely to occur within targets such as the superior colliculus. For example, we suggest that, in non-mammalian vertebrates, inputs from multiple sensory modalities compete to control the motor outputs of the superior colliculus. Following our theme that useful circuits once evolved often remain functional as evolution proceeds, we suggest that this is the case for layer 5 projection neurons. That is, their appropriate synaptic outputs are nullified subcortically via circuitry evolved for that purpose. If this occurs, the circuitry that underlies this is a complete mystery, but the question should be raised. It also seems plausible that cortical areas not actively involved in controlling behavior at a given time, such as auditory areas during attention to visual stimuli, may have reduced overall responsiveness; in particular, this may apply to their layer 5 subcortical projections, which would contribute to the filtering. Nonetheless this suggestion further emphasizes the point that attention is not strictly a cortical phenomenon.
Evolution and Efference Copies
Our brains are constantly fed sensory information about the environment. Often, a proper and immediate response to any change in the environment can be the difference between death and survival. A problem that needs to be resolved to do this as quickly as possible is the disambiguation of changes actually caused by the environment versus those induced by self-motion. This problem exists for any organism that moves within its environment. This dilemma was recognized as early as the nineteenth century by Helmholtz (Helmholtz, 1866) who asserted that what we now know as efference copy (also known as corollary discharge; Wurtz, 2018) has to be a feature of brains of all motile animals. It was not until 1950 that efference copies were independently demonstrated experimentally in fishes (Sperry, 1950) and flies (von Holst and Mittelstaedt, 1950). Efference copies are now believed to be ubiquitous in motile animals as Helmholtz suggested.
A simple example helps to illustrate this issue. Consider the action of sliding one's palm across a table surface. The sensory information reflects relative movement of the hand versus the tabletop, which by itself does not allow one to determine if this is due to hand movement alone, movement of the tabletop alone, or a combination of both. Thus, the sensory message alone does not allow a determination of whether or not the environment has changed (i.e., possible movement of the tabletop). The problem is solved by efference copies.
Neuronal substrates for efference copies
Figure 3A schematically illustrates the neuronal substrate for efference copy. When a motor command is sent from an executive source (e.g., certain layer 5 subcortical projections, tectospinal neurons, etc.) to ultimately activate motoneurons, an exact copy of that command is sent back into the appropriate sensorimotor processing center, which then creates a forward model of what to expect from the anticipated movement. The simplest prediction would be that sensory input from the subsequent behavior resulting from that movement could be explained entirely from that movement. That is, the relative motion of the hand across the table top leads to sensory input explained entirely by the hand movement. If the comparison of the new sensory information matches the forward model, no environmental change is indicated, but if there is disagreement, then an immediate indicator of environmental change can be recognized.

Figure 3.
Schemas illustrating concept of efference copy. A, With efference copy. As a message is sent from a sensorimotor processing stream that eventually affects motoneuron activity (Motor Command), a copy of this message, which is the efference copy, is sent back into the processing stream to then create a forward model or prediction of the effects of the imminent movement. The evoked behavior creates a new sensory input that is compared against the forward model to determine whether or not a change in the environment contributed to the evoked sensation. B, Without efference copy. When any action is taken, the combination of evoked sensory stimuli, including both potential changes in the environment and information regarding the movement itself, such as changes in joint angle, etc. This complex combination of sensations must go through a second stage of processing to separate those new sensations caused by self-motion versus those caused by actual changes in the environment. Compared with the scenario in A, having no efference copy delays an evaluation of whether the environment has changed. See text for details.
Figure 3B shows that, without efference copy, the evaluation of sensory information needed to disambiguate environmental changes from any self-induced changes takes an extra step and a further delay that could prove fatal to the organism. In the example of moving the hand across a table top, without efference copy and a forward model for instant analysis, the brain must first gather all of the relevant new sensory information—motion of the table surface versus the palm, changed joint angles of the arm and wrist, etc.—and then use this information to determine what, if anything, is changing in the environment.
Branching axons
One challenge in creating an efference copy is to ensure that the copy of the motor command is accurate, without error. Arguably, the best way to make certain that an exact copy of a message is transmitted from one neuron to multiple targets, meaning the same temporal pattern of action potentials, is via a branching axon (Cox et al., 2000; Raastad and Shepherd, 2003). The message is the same, but due to potential differences in synaptic properties and/or postsynaptic circuitry, the copies may typically evoke different postsynaptic effects (see below).
Branching axons were first emphasized as a major feature of neuronal projections by Cajal (Cajal, 1911), and an example of his work is shown in Figure 4A. Here he demonstrated that every primary afferent entering the spinal cord branches multiple times, with one branch heading toward the ventral horn and another ascending to the brain. Branching axons seem a particularly ubiquitous feature of the central nervous system, and we are unaware of any clear examples of axons that lack one or more branches to innervate multiple neurons, usually in different central targets.
