Figure 4.
Branching axons and efference copy. A, Annotated version of Cajal's drawing (Cajal, 1911) showing that each primary afferent axon to the spinal cord branches, with one branch heading to the spinal gray matter, and the other, to the brain. The red arrows indicate branch points. B, Modern textbook view of A. The axonal branch targeting a motoneuron (e.g., for a tendon reflex) can be viewed as a motor message. The ascending branch (blue) carries an exact copy of that message to the brain. This one message can be interpreted by some postsynaptic targets as a sensory message and by others as a copy of a motor message, which is the definition of an efference copy. See text for details. C, Schematic analogy to demonstrate that a single message such as the ascending branch in B can be interpreted in multiple ways, including as an efference copy. See text for details. D, Any circuit creating a motor command must also create an efference copy of that command. Such circuits, as a result of evolution, exist at multiple levels of the central nervous system. Three such levels and examples are shown—spinal cord, midbrain, and cortex.
Example of an efference copy involving spinal circuitry
Figure 4B shows a textbook version of Figure 4A. The primary afferent, as indicated in Cajal's drawing, branches: one innervates the ventral horn and the other ascends toward the brain. A conventional view of this is that the branch to the ventral horn activates motoneurons via a spinal reflex, and the other provides the brain with sensory information, such as a change in a joint angle or skin indentation. However, since all axonal branches carry the same message, the ascending branch carries an exact copy of the motor message sent to the ventral horn. Such replication defines an efference copy.
However, Figure 4B further suggests that the ascending message can be both sensory and act as an efference copy. This can happen because the singular message innervates multiple targets that treat the information differently (e.g., as sensory or efference copy). Figure 4C provides a very schematic analogy that may help to illustrate this point of multiple meanings for one message. Imagine an army unit is placed to protect a strategic bridge. The unit is sent a message from its local commander: “The enemy is advancing towards the river. Have your squad destroy the bridge.” This message can be seen as a motor message, since it commands action. An exact copy of this motor message is sent to area headquarters. This copy serves two functions: one can be seen as sensory, because it updates headquarters on battlefield actions; and the other can be seen as an efference copy, because it creates an expectation that the bridge will be destroyed, and a failure to do so means something unexpected has occurred (in the environment).
Example of efference copy involving cortex
Conventional views of sensorimotor processing involving cortex rarely include efference copies. Figure 5A shows a fairly common textbook perspective. There are two problems with this depiction:
For an incoming signal from the environment, which might pose a threat, to have any behavioral effect requires all the circuitry from the thalamic input through the cortical hierarchy until an executive motor region is activated to respond with an appropriate behavior. This would take too long. Any time a new sensory receptor or peripheral sensory process evolves, it will have no survival value if it lacks a fairly immediate motor output.
Whenever efference copies are considered, their anatomical basis is often ignored or only abstractly suggested. When suggested, a common depiction is a singular efference copy at the end of a sensorimotor circuit. But if the motor command starts in cortex, the efference copy, to be effective, needs to reach cortex. This scheme offers no path fast enough to provide a useful forward model.

Figure 5.
Views of sensorimotor processing involving cortex. A, Conventional view typical of textbook accounts. Here, sensory information is relayed by thalamus to sensory cortex after which processing occurs strictly within cortex via an ascending hierarchy that ends at an executive level, and from this top level, a motor command is evoked in response to the sensory stimulus. Note that this schema has a single cortical entry and exit point for the processing, that most of thalamus (question marks) are not involved, and that any efference copy suggested is usually placed at or near the end of this circuit. B, Alternate view. Note that a bottleneck is created by considerable convergence of layer 5 input to the subcortical motor center, that most of thalamus ignored in A is involved in transthalamic processing, and that the message relayed through them can be interpreted as an efference copy. See text for details.
There are two other problems not seen in Figure 5A but dealt with in Figure 5B. First, Figure 5A fails to illustrate the “bottleneck” problem for cortical control of behavior that requires some filtering of cortical motor commands through subcortical links. This is clearly shown in Figure 5B. Second, if a cortical area issues a motor command from a layer 5 projection, this command should produce an efference copy that is immediately sent to cortex. Virtually all of these layer 5 projections branch to innervate subcortical sites: the vast majority branch to innervate thalamus and one or more extrathalamic motor sites, like the superior colliculus, although some innervate only thalamus or extrathalamic sites (Guillery and Sherman, 2011; Sherman and Guillery, 2013; Economo et al., 2018; Usrey and Sherman, 2021; Sherman and Usrey, 2024). Indeed, all areas so far studied produce layer 5 axons that branch to innervate both the thalamus and superior colliculus. This means that cortical processing can produce a relatively early response to a sensory stimulus, since the earliest thalamocortical targets can generate a motor command via layer 5 projections. Furthermore, those axons that branch to thalamus plus a motor center indicate that the thalamic branch carries information that can be processed as an efference copy, and this is rapidly fed back to cortex.
These thalamic regions targeted by layer 5 inputs are called higher order because they relay information already in cortex, whereas those innervated for relay by subcortical sites, like the retinogeniculate projection, are known as first order (Sherman and Guillery, 2013; Usrey and Sherman, 2021; Sherman and Usrey, 2024). Figure 5B also shows that the layer 5 projections to thalamus initiate cortico-thalamo-cortical routes, and all such transthalamic circuits are paralleled by direct connections between these cortical areas.
It is also worth pointing out that, whereas we have emphasized the motor consequences of the layer 5 projections, they also contribute to sensory and other types of processing. One example is the transthalamic circuitry initiated by these axons. Another is the observation that many of the subcortical targets appear not to be involved directly in motor actions.
Another feature emphasized by Figure 5B is that all cortical areas have such a layer 5 motor output. Thus, as an area lower in the hierarchy sends out a motor command, an efference copy is sent up to the next level. Furthermore, the distinction between sensory and motor cortex is misleading. Indeed, virtually all circuits in the brain can be viewed as sensorimotor, and this applies to all cortical areas.
Hierarchies of efference copy produced by evolution
We have emphasized two aspects of brain evolution. First, much of our behavior is controlled by subcortical circuits independently of cortex. This means that subcortical motor commands are generated, and these must be associated with efference copies. Second, useful circuits, once evolved, tend to remain and function even as newer, more advanced circuits are added to the brain.
Put together, this means that, as we move through our environment with some behaviors purely subcortically controlled, and others, cortically, we produce a stack of efference copies at different hierarchical levels that reflect evolutionary history. Figure 4D provides a highly simplified, schematic, and reduced view of this. An important and unresolved question is: how does the brain keep track of all of these efferent copies?
Implications of Proposed Attentional Filtering on Transthalamic Circuitry
We have speculated above that attentional demands involving cortex require that layer 5 projections to subcortical motor targets from cortical areas not involved at the time in controlling behavior must be suppressed or filtered out. If so, a likely result is that these layer 5 neurons are less responsive. Since these neurons are the source of transthalamic circuitry via branching axons (Fig. 5B), it follows that communication between cortical areas through these transthalamic routes may occur effectively only when the appropriate cortical areas are involved in attention and/or other cognitive tasks affecting behavior—when corticofugal commands are active—rather than during periods of purely intracortical processing. Perhaps related to this is the observation that the higher order relays involved in transthalamic processing and attention (reviewed in Boshra and Kastner, 2022) are heavily innervated by modulatory inputs that can affect their relay function, including shutting them down (Sherman and Guillery, 2013; Usrey and Sherman, 2021; Koster and Sherman, 2024, 2026). An important implication is that testing of transthalamic function in experimental paradigms may reveal little unless the cortical areas in question are actively engaged in an appropriate behavioral task.
Evolution and Computational Neuroscience Models
Much of computational neuroscience is directed at understanding brain circuits. As noted above, the study of neuroscience operates chiefly through a corticocentric bias, and this applies to brain modeling. In many such models, cortex is treated as a largely self-contained computational system in which perception, decision-making, and motor planning occur within cortical networks before a final output stage sends commands to downstream structures. Motor output is therefore often treated as the terminal stage of processing rather than as an integral part of the computation itself. While valuable, these models do not address the issue we raise here: that understanding how the brain controls behavior requires models informed by evolution and that involve circuitry operating at multiple hierarchical levels. This dictates the inclusion of much subcortical circuitry.
An evolutionary perspective suggests that models of brain function should incorporate the hierarchical organization of motor control systems, including spinal central pattern generators, brainstem command circuits, midbrain sensorimotor structures such as the superior colliculus, and corticofugal pathways that interact with these systems. In mammals, cortex influences behavior through layer 5 corticofugal projections that engage these older motor systems. Modeling cortical function without including these pathways therefore omits a critical component of the system.
Whereas there are numerous attempts to model certain corticofugal circuitry, these are mostly limited to projections to thalamus or basal ganglia (Schroll and Hamker, 2013; Papale and Hooks, 2018; Topalidou et al., 2018; Haar and Donchin, 2020). None that we have found addresses the circuitry suggested by Figures 1C, 2, 4D, or 5B, in which cortical outputs interact with multiple levels of subcortical motor control. Developing such models represents an important challenge for future computational neuroscience.
Concluding Remarks
Understanding brain functioning needs to take into account its evolutionary history. Evolution has produced a most complex instrument that can be seen as a reflection of a process that generally retains useful brain circuits as newer, more efficient ones evolve. That is, newer ones do not replace older ones but instead are added into the mix, creating a hierarchy of brain circuits that reflects evolutionary history. Functioning of the brain requires that circuits evolved at different times and reflected in these hierarchical levels must interact constantly to ensure appropriate responses to environmental challenges.
Most neuroscience research tends to focus on the operation of isolated brain structures, which, while clearly valuable and timely, do not address this larger issue of brain functioning. For instance, understanding how cortex controls behavior requires how it interacts with older subcortical circuitry via its layer 5 projections. Yet studies of cortex are mostly limited to studying isolated cortical areas or collections of such areas. Furthermore, neuroscience has a corticocentric bias that often overlooks how subcortical structures routinely control behaviors independently of cortex, and when cortex is involved, it must play nice with many active subcortical control regions.
We argue that taking this evolutionary perspective on brain functioning generally, and that of cortex specifically, is a sine qua non for understanding the neuronal bases of such functions as attention and efference copies, among many others. After all, the theory of evolution is the basis of our understanding of all biology and needs to be more fully incorporated into the study of neuroscience.

Abstract

Evolutionary Divergence in Dopamine Regulation between Rodents and Primates and Its Implications for Neurobiological Research
Letizia Zanetti
Journal of Neuroscience 3 June 2026, 46 (22) e0567252026; https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.0567-25.2026
Letizia Zanetti
Department of Psychiatry, Columbia University Irving Medical Center, New York, New York 10032
Find this author on Google Scholar
Find this author on PubMed
Search for this author on this site
Article
Info & Metrics
eLetters
PDF
Dopamine orchestrates essential functions such as sleep, movement, and learning, as well as shaping complex behaviors including reward motivation, decision-making, and social interaction. For this reason, many studies have been conducted to understand the role of dopamine in these processes. Often this work has performed using mice because of their availability and cost-effectiveness and because their brain structure and function are generally homologous to those of humans and nonhuman primates, particularly in midbrain–basal ganglia circuitry. Moreover, direct species comparisons have demonstrated similar dorsal-to-ventral gradients of dopamine release in the striatum of rodents and nonhuman primates (Cragg et al., 2000; Calipari et al., 2012), suggesting a conserved system for action selection and reward processing. Yet, species differences exist. For instance, comparative neuroanatomical studies showed that whereas in nonhuman primates, the caudate and putamen are two distinct nuclei; in mice these structures are fused into a single caudate putamen, or striatum, which is located beneath the cortex (Zeiss, 2005). Moreover, the organization of the mesocortical dopaminergic system is notably more complex in primates, with larger, more organized terminal fields, distinct neuropeptide colocalization, and earlier prenatal development (Berger et al., 1991).
In light of these and other interspecies differences in dopaminergic pathway organization, Shin et al. (2025) recently compared dopamine release and its regulation in mice and macaques. They used two complementary methods to monitor dopamine release in dorsal striatum brain slices. The first method, fast-scan cyclic voltammetry (FSCV), allows quantification of electrically evoked dopamine release, but it has relatively low sensitivity and limited temporal resolution (10 Hz). To overcome these limitations, the authors used photometry, using the genetically encoded fluorescent dopamine sensors GRAB-DA1m and dLight1.3b. These sensors, in which GFP is fused to the intracellular loop 3 (ICL3) of the D1 receptors, respectively, allow real-time, high-resolution detection (up to 5 kHz), with the caveats that their expression may interfere with endogenous receptor binding and they have a more limited dynamic range than FSCV.
In both mice and monkeys, stronger stimulation led to correspondingly larger dopamine signals recorded with either detection method. Furthermore, dopamine release levels varied across striatal subregions in both species, with the nucleus accumbens (NAc) exhibiting significantly lower peak dopamine concentrations than the putamen/dorsolateral striatum (DLS) and caudate/dorsomedial striatum (DMS) but no significant difference between DLS and DMS. Despite these similarities, however, the overall evoked dopamine release was lower in monkeys than in mice, in contrast to previous comparison between guinea pig and marmoset DLS, which showed higher release in monkeys than in guinea pigs (Cragg, 2003).
To further investigate these species differences, the authors examined the role of two key transmitters that influence striatal dopamine release: GABA and acetylcholine. GABA, which is released by most striatal neurons, inhibits dopamine release by acting on GABAA receptors located on presynaptic and postsynaptic terminals of VTA and NAc dopaminergic neurons; notably, dopamine axons can also corelease GABA, enabling a form of autoinhibition (Patel et al., 2024). Acetylcholine, which is released by cholinergic interneurons in the striatum, controls dopamine release by binding to receptors on dopaminergic axons, and its effects vary depending on receptor class. In the striatum, nicotinic acetylcholine receptors (nAChRs) on dopamine axons activate rapid dopamine release, whereas muscarinic acetylcholinergic receptors can either facilitate or inhibit release, depending on the subtype expressed. Importantly, the subunit composition of nAChRs strongly influences their functional properties. β2-containing nAChRs are evolutionarily ancient and broadly expressed across the brain, including the striatum, where they contribute to cognitive and reward-related processes. In contrast, α6-containing nAChRs emerged later in evolution and exhibit a far more selective expression pattern and are largely restricted to midbrain dopaminergic neurons and their striatal axons, where they typically assemble as α6β2 or α6β2β3 nAChRs.
Shin et al. (2025) first examined the role of GABA. They found that a combination of GABAA and GABAB receptor blockers increased evoked dopamine release in both mice and monkeys but with a stronger effect in primate striatum than in mouse. To dissect possible interactions between GABAergic and cholinergic regulation of dopamine release, the authors applied GABA blockers alone or after application of a β2-nAChR antagonist to investigate whether GABAergic regulation actions involved acetylcholine. Under these conditions, GABA receptor antagonists still produced a substantial increase in dopamine release in macaque striatum, suggesting that GABA-mediated modulation of dopamine release is independent of acetylcholine modulation in macaques. In mouse, however GABA-blocker–mediated disinhibition was dampened in the presence of the β2-nAChR antagonist.
Finally, Shin et al. (2025) blocked either β2-nAChRs or α6-nAChRs. Blocking either receptor subtype decreased evoked dopamine release in both mice and monkeys, but the magnitude of the effects differed. Specifically, blocking β2-nAChRs in mice suppressed dopamine release more than in macaques. In contrast, blocking α6-nAChRs had a much lower effect in mice than monkeys, with near-complete suppression of dopamine release in macaques. To test whether this difference reflected species-specific pharmacological interactions, the authors sequentially applied an α6-nAChR antagonist followed by one for β2-nAChRs. The treatment produced no additional decrease in dopamine release in macaques but enhanced release suppression in mice. Together, these findings show that dopamine release in macaques is regulated almost exclusively through α6-containing nAChRs, while additional nAChR subtypes contribute in mice.
In summary, Shin et al. (2025) showed that regional differences in dopamine release and its regulation are partially conserved across mouse and primates, but significant divergence exists. Cholinergic modulation of dopamine release occurs across the striatum in both mice and macaques, with a notably stronger effect in the dorsal striatum than in the ventral NAc. However, macaques appear to rely almost exclusively on α6-containing nAChRs, whereas mice also utilize non-α6-containing subtypes. Similarly, GABAergic modulation of the cholinergic system is species-specific. In mice, the GABA-blocker–mediated disinhibition is dampened by β2-nAChR antagonists, suggesting that GABA partially regulates dopamine indirectly by modulating striatal cholinergic interneurons. This exists alongside a direct mechanism: using selective optical stimulation of dopamine axons, robust disinhibition still occurs in mice upon GABAergic blockade even in the absence of acetylcholine (Patel et al., 2024). Such a multilayered system found in mice is lost in macaques, in which β2-nAChRs appear to play a smaller role, suggesting a shift toward a more direct and independent GABAergic “brake” on the dopamine axons themselves. Together, this shows that GABAergic inhibition of dopamine release is also conserved across species, but it is more pronounced in primates. This may be due to higher GABA receptor expression on dopamine axons or other species-specific traits, such as a higher proportion of GABAergic interneurons in primates than in mice (Bakken et al., 2021).
The shift toward stronger GABAergic control in primates might reflect species-specific adaptations: while small prey animals like mice benefit from a reactive dopaminergic system that enables rapid, minimally gated behavioral responses, primates have larger brains and longer lifespans, which require more precise, energy-efficient modulation of dopamine signaling exerted through GABAergic control to support complex cognition and behavioral flexibility.
Another possible contributor to species difference in dopamine release is differences in dopamine tissue content, because the amount of dopamine available helps determine how much can be released. Interestingly, prior research has demonstrated that marmoset putamen contains approximately twice the dopamine content of the guinea pig (Wetherell et al., 1989). Therefore, accurate quantification of dopamine levels in mice and macaques using high-performance liquid chromatography would provide additional insight into the species-specific differences in dopamine storage, release, and striatal innervation observed.
The study by Shin et al. (2025) is important because it sheds light on dopamine release dynamics in nonhuman primates, which have critical translational relevance. While rodents remain indispensable to biomedical research, their evolutionary distance from primates (divergence ∼96 million years ago) limits their use to model human physiology and pathology. Primate models offer distinct advantages due to their close phylogenetic relationship to humans, especially in the areas of cognition and aging. Assuming that dopamine regulation in humans is similar to that in primates, the findings by Shin et al. (2025) carry important implications for mouse modeling of conditions in which dopamine release in the striatum is affected, e.g., substance use disorder and Parkinson's disease (PD). A major challenge in modeling PD in mice has been the reliable replication of motor phenotypes, which often fail to manifest even with selective neurodegeneration of dopaminergic neurons in the substantia nigra. In this context, the work of Shin et al. demonstrates that a stronger baseline cholinergic tone in mice may act as a confounding factor, potentially masking motor deficits associated with nigrostriatal degeneration. Such a regulation of striatal dopamine release also has implications for pharmacological studies: a stronger baseline cholinergic tone in mice may cause researchers to underestimate the compensation from nAChR-specific or GABAR-mediated modulation, either making a candidate drug appear more or less effective in mice than it would be in humans. For example, cholinesterase inhibitors or nAChR modulators may be better tolerated in mice, where the cholinergic system is more diffuse and redundant, yet increase the higher risk of side effects such as dyskinesia in humans, where α6-containing nAChRs are more selectively and prominently expressed. Overall, the findings of Shin et al. underscore the importance of accounting for species-specific differences when interpreting parkinsonian motor phenotypes, highlight the limitations of relying on mouse models only, and point to the potential therapeutic relevance of α6-nAChR agonists.
In conclusion, the findings by Shin et al. (2025) show that the regulation of dopamine release in the striatum differs between mice and primates. The work thus underscores the necessity of examining species differences in neurobiological research. This should help improve our understanding of dopaminergic circuits and diseases in which they are affected.
